I picked up the ade.

The sweetness jolted me awake.

Whether it was the sugar or some kind of adrenaline awakening, my mind stayed sharply focused all morning.

My plans, the tasks I had to accomplish within the month, all flooded in.

‘First, I keep my head down.’

Since that incident with Agent Choi, I’d lay low for about a week, avoid drawing attention from the Disaster Response Agency, and use that time to lay some light groundwork before making a move.

For now, timing mattered more than duration in my plan.

But—

There was one dilemma.

‘...Should I tell Yeongeun-ssi about this?’

It felt right to share my situation, but it was hard to gauge just how much to say—too fine a line.

How would Yeongeun-ssi respond if I told her I had one month to finish everything?

Would it even be right to tell her about Director Ho’s malicious plan—how he deliberately sent in spies expecting us to be discovered?

Would knowing help her do anything about it...?

“......”

Maybe it’d be easier to work as a spy if she stayed unaware—less chance of slipping up.

My stomach hurt.

Felt like acid.

‘No. It’s okay.’

Yeongeun-ssi only had one more month left to work, too.

If I just pulled this off, brought Director Ho the information he wanted in the format he needed, I could also claim Yeongeun-ssi’s share in the process.

Then it would all be over, cleanly.

‘Good.’

Let’s do this.

“Phew.”

I set down the now-empty ade.

Next to me, Agent Cheongdong, sipping a vanilla latte while reviewing some documents, suddenly spoke.

“That doll isn’t here today.”

“......!”

“You know, the little bunny plush you call Brown.”

“...It’s here.”

I pulled the plush out from my jacket and showed it briefly, then stuffed it back in.

“Uh, so, is the doll doing well?”

“Probably.”

I quickly pulled the hand that had touched the plush out of my pocket.

That’s when it happened.

BEEPBEEPBEEPBEEP!

“...!”

An obnoxious alarm blared from Agent Cheongdong’s wrist device.

An alert.

‘Rescue request!’

“Stand by.”

Agent Cheongdong tossed his documents aside and stood up.

He checked the code on the pager to confirm the type of ghost tale involved, then started preparing.

“...It’s a tale that requires exactly two personnel for rescue. We’ll be entering together.”

“Yes.”

I rose immediately as well.

The past few weeks of deployments had conditioned my body to respond automatically—ironically grounding me in reality.

“The dispatch point’s pretty far. Normally, the call would go to the nearest branch... but this one was made through the direct HQ line.”

“Where is it?”

“Asan-si, Chungcheongnam-do. The supernatural disaster in question... is on the large side, spatially speaking, so there’s a lot to review.”

Agent Cheongdong quickly pulled out a small booklet from the shelf.

“This is a manual-slash-tip guide our team put together. Read it while we’re in transit.”

“Yes.”

As soon as Agent Cheongdong forwarded the call to off-duty members, we left HQ immediately.

And our mode of transport...

“The rescue window for this incident isn’t too tight, so we’ll be using public transportation.”

...was high-speed rail.

Departing from Seoul Station.

To Mokpo.

“......”

“Agent Podo?”

“Ah, no, nothing.”

I sat down in the train seat.

Don’t panic.

The Tamra-bound high-speed train incident was a special case.

It doesn’t happen every time, and Baekilmong Corporation is surely monitoring the occurrence conditions for research by now...

It’s fine.

I can do this.

Don’t get anxious.

If I lose it here, I won’t be able to do anything. I’d end up jumping at every convenience store entrance, unable to ride elevators, unable to step onto subway trains....

Departure

The train departed without incident.

I was fine.

Distract yourself.

Right. The manual.

“There’s a prohibition on it—if you’re not with the Disaster Response Agency, the contents will appear different, so feel free to read it.”

Agent Cheongdong kindly explained as I opened the booklet. Good. I’d focus on the ghost tale manual and tips, and figure out the rescue strategy.

I turned the page—

—I really put effort into this one, Podo, so use it well lololol

“...!!”

“What’s wr... ah.”

Agent Cheongdong frowned, then sighed.

“It’s mostly written by Agent Choi. He left a lot of useless comments. ...I think he added more after you joined.”

I stared blankly as I flipped through it.

Older-looking handwriting began to appear.

—Newbie welcome! Hyunmu Team 1’s ace, Agent Choi’s super tips extravaganza!

└Please stop writing things like this.

└Agent Cheongdong’s milder tips are included too.

└...Ha.

Different handwriting styles, different pens.

—Read the next page thoroughly. It’s an introduction to the supernatural disaster—might be hard, but treat it like a novel.

My eyes trembled.

A new section began: a ghost tale manual and tips handwritten directly by Agent Choi, which hadn’t been included in Darkness Exploration Records.

This help guide is entirely for the smooth rescue of human lives in Registration Code 1006PSYA.1941.A06, a.k.a. “Jisanso Offering Ritual.”

Our ever-strict agency classified this supernatural disaster as a “Type: Shaped-Breaking,” but the writer strongly argued for a higher grade.

Even if the rescue itself isn’t difficult, the disaster cannot be fully terminated, and multiple dokkaebi testimonies suggest the presence of something more evil inside.

So don’t let your guard down, and always be prepared for dispatch during lunar April and May!

“This way.”

We got off at Cheonan-Asan Station.

Then we pedaled off using government bicycles stored at the station.

“It’s about thirty minutes by bike.”

And it was, just as he said.

Location of Occurrence: Jisan Village, ■■-ri, Asan-si

As we left the station and moved away from the city center, winding unpaved roads emerged, and vibrations from the bike saddle hit hard.

In the distance, a small village came into view.

A typical rural village.

Old countryside houses and tile-roofed homes patched with modern renovations, utility wires crisscrossing above. The dirt roads were paved only around the village itself.

This village is easily accessible with the right transport, but is closed off to outsiders due to its family-based residency (no real estate listings; relocation or infiltration impossible).

However, during the “Jisan Festival” period, outsiders are welcomed and may participate in the local celebrations.

“There. ...It’s begun.”

A banner came into sight.

Jisan Festival

May you be blessed by Jisan

This very festival was the supernatural phenomenon.

“This way.”

We parked the bikes at the edge of the village. Nearby, we spotted several vehicles with “Seoul” or “Gyeonggi” license plates parked haphazardly.

“......”

“Remember that.”

I nodded.

Then Agent Cheongdong and I stepped into the village.

Hiyahhhhhhh!

The closer we got, the more people appeared.

It wasn’t a massive crowd like a city festival, but there were traditional music troupes here and there, people snapping photos, others seated and enjoying pork slices and soup provided by the village.

And also those looking bored, fiddling with their smartphones.

※Quick tip before entry!

Absolutely do not wear Disaster Response Agency jackets; bring earplugs, matches, and bells.

Above all...

Do not sit among the villagers.

“Eugh, what is this? Feels shady.”

“Wait. They’re doing a raffle, apparently.”

We sat beside two people who clearly looked like disinterested outsiders.

The food was served quickly.

“Please, have a bite! May you be blessed by Jisan!”

A village woman smiled at me.

She looked to be around my age, and had what looked like bird feathers pinned to her chest.

Identifying villagers at the Jisan Festival is simple:

If they’re wearing a brooch made of feathers, they’re locals.

—I snuck one and had it analyzed; it’s rooster feathers. Dokkaebi were disgusted.

Voices echoed around.

“Oh my, you’re from Mapo? My son works in Mapo, Seoul too!”

“This is a no-smoking area. Absolutely no smoking!”

“Have a drink. Homemade, strong but smooth.”

Villagers struck up conversations with outsiders, acted kindly, offered alcohol.

It all felt like a warm-hearted countryside village.

Except that someone was about to die.

I suppressed a small sigh and looked at the food bowl.

Festival food is safe to eat. (Honestly, it’s tasty.)

But personally, I’d avoid soup-based dishes. You’re better off not knowing why.

Not like I had any appetite anyway.

“...Thank you for the food.”

“Thank you.”

Agent Cheongdong and I only went through the motions with our spoons.

Hiyahhhhhhh!

The music continued through the village.

It sounded like traditional folk music, and yet the rhythm felt strangely unfamiliar....

—Ah. That’s due to its purpose. What was that music composed for, you see!

“...!”

I reflexively glanced down at my pocket.

—Ah! So you can hear me now, friend!

—Haha, I was briefly flustered when conversation became impossible. But it’s alright! If you were worried this cotton-stuffed body might fall off, I’d have stayed silent.

—But here we are, chatting once again!

The supernatural disaster.

Now that we were inside the ghost tale, the plush toy could speak again.

—Don’t worry. Even when I couldn’t speak, I was always listening to you. Like a good friend.

.......

—Oh, and no need to apologize for leaving me in your jacket yesterday. Quite the opposite! It was better to have something covering me in that horrible room.

‘...I see. That’s good then.’

I turned my gaze back to the food.

—...Friend? You look more tired than usual. Could it be... leftover fatigue from last night’s adventure?

‘Maybe.’

—Then how about we head home later and watch some TV together? Maybe that cartoon or variety show you liked.

—And then you can sleep soundly. Brown here will tell you the most delightful bedtime stories to ensure sweet dreams!

.......

—Friend?

‘Can I focus on work for now? I don’t know much about this tale, so...’

—Ah, of course!

And finally, the plush went quiet.

Thank goodness.

I turned my head and, like Agent Cheongdong, began collecting information.

Who had sent the rescue signal?

And—

Which among the villagers was the appropriate person?

The Jisan Festival lasts four days. No matter when the rescue signal came in, as long as the festival isn’t over, the person can still be saved.

Observe the villagers closely, and find someone who looks sullen—then request to stay the night!

—I didn’t write down their description in case it confused you. But whatever happens!

Never trust someone who strikes up a friendly conversation as an outsider, Podo.

—You know those freaks who lure kids with candy? It’s just like that lol

I kept that in mind as I scanned the villagers.

Let’s see... someone who looked the least friendly...

“...??”

Wait a second.

I jerked my head to the side.

There was a familiar face.

A sullen-faced young man in his 20s standing among the villagers.

Wearing a medical eye patch. A jerk from Baekilmong Corporation.

‘...Baek Saheon!’

That bastard was here.

With a rooster feather pinned to his chest.

And just beyond Baek Saheon—

I spotted a woman with a tired expression, seated among the table of outsiders being approached by villagers.

Wearing gloves. Casually scanning her surroundings.

A face I knew all too well.

“......”

It was Assistant Manager Eunhaje.

Chapter 196

To begin with, a fairly rational guess immediately came to mind regarding why Baek Saheon was in this bizarre countryside village.

‘...Baek Saheon was on that Tamra-bound train!’

It had been the same high-speed train to Mokpo that I’d boarded today.

‘Was this his destination back then too?’

It had looked like he was heading to his family home—so was this village... Baek Saheon’s hometown?

It was a strange feeling.

The scene of a quaint, heartwarming countryside festival and Baek Saheon felt weirdly mismatched.

Even more so if this village festival was a cult-like ghost tale centered around a strange little society.

“Take that over there!”

“Ah, yes.”

Even now, Baek Saheon, wearing his usual sullen face, quietly nodded at another villager’s command and carried dishes—helping out with chores.

And yet...

While observing the villagers carefully, find the most sour-looking person and ask to stay overnight!

According to Agent Choi’s comment, was that guy actually one of the eligible candidates?

It was an off-putting, eerie situation, but at least I could piece together the context up to that point.

The real question was...

Why was she here?

‘...Assistant Manager Eunhaje.’

I subtly turned my head again, doing my best not to draw Agent Cheongdong’s attention, and confirmed her figure.

Assistant Manager Eunhaje was helping herself to more boiled pork, drinking soju alongside it.

As casually as she would in a company cafeteria.

“......”

Seeing that casual, nonchalant expression and movement gave me an odd feeling.

Unconsciously, my gaze lingered in her direction—

Our eyes met.

...!

At that moment, Assistant Manager Eunhaje casually picked up her pork plate and soju bottle—

And began walking over.

......?!

Why—why is she coming this way?

Then she dropped naturally into the seat across from us and began speaking to Agent Cheongdong, as if it were nothing.

“Bit too much pork on my plate—would you like a piece?”

“......Who are you?”

“I’m a reporter. Heard there was a local festival, so I came to see if there was any story material... mind if I ask you a few questions while I watch?”

Deputy Manager...!

Assistant Manager Eunhaje smoothly pretended not to recognize me, chatting up Agent Cheongdong like an overworked reporter.

The look in Agent Cheongdong’s eyes froze. It was the expression of someone suspecting a bothersome infiltrator.

“I don’t really have anything to say.”

“Then at least let me ramble a bit. From what I saw yesterday, the festival’s method of luring in outsiders seems to be...”

DANG DANG DANG DANG DANG!!

“...!!”

Our heads turned at the deafening crash of jingles and drums.

The music stopped, and from among the people performing folk plays, a kindly looking middle-aged man climbed up and shouted into a megaphone.

[Everyone! It’s raffle time now! Enjoy the prizes and be blessed by Jisan!]

“Right. That’s the thing—they do a raffle every festival day.”

During the four-day festival period, it seemed they held a prize drawing every day.

The prizes were mostly local farm goods, though occasionally handcrafted items or jewelry would appear.

In the case of jewelry, the branch suspected they were personal items from missing persons.

The villagers spread out with raffle boxes in hand. All around, people were excitedly reacting—whether they won or lost. We even saw someone cheer after drawing “gold” and receive a pair of gold earrings.

“Oh, that’s lucky. Though those earrings look kinda secondhand.”

“......”

“......”

“Probably just something someone donated for the festival.”

None of us replied.

And then, someone began walking toward us with a raffle box.

A sullen-faced man wearing a medical eye patch.

‘Ba—Baek Saheon.’

Was this some kind of Baekilmong staff reunion? We were on the verge of a three-way face-off.

The bigger issue—

Agent Cheongdong recognized Baek Saheon’s face.

“Wait...!”

Agent Cheongdong’s expression froze as he realized the man from the serial killer villa—the “coworker” of mine.

Then he glanced back and forth between me and Baek Saheon. I shook my head.

“It’s fine.”

“...Understood.”

Agent Cheongdong calmed down quickly.

He was confident that my identity hadn’t been exposed.

Because—

They wouldn’t recognize us anyway.

During the festival period, villagers do not recognize acquaintances visiting from outside.

They treat everyone purely as welcomed guests—there are testimonies of people who noticed this sense of dissonance and managed to escape.

—I even interrogated them thinking they were faking it, but they genuinely didn’t recognize me. Seems like a weird prohibition’s in place. HQ has no data, so if I’m not around, just let it pass.

Baek Saheon approached.

His face still looked as unpleasant as ever, and even though our eyes met, he showed no reaction.

He simply—

“Please, draw one.”

—extended the raffle box toward our table.

“......”

“I heard the prizes are pretty good this year... oh, thanks.”

Assistant Manager Eunhaje casually reached in and drew a stick. On the end, it said: “Rice.”

“Your turn now.”

I met eyes with Agent Cheongdong.

Do not refuse to draw. Refusing means no villager will offer you lodging. If you want to avoid calling in a panic rescue from HQ, don’t forget!

“Excuse me. Aren’t you drawing?”

“Ah. I will.”

I reached into the rooster-shaped raffle box made of wood. It felt strangely damp and humid.

And what I pulled was—

“A miss.”

Whew.

Agent Cheongdong also reached in cautiously, and drew a stick that likewise read “miss.”

“Too bad. Would’ve been nice if you at least took something home.”

Assistant Manager Eunhaje pointed toward the village square and the middle-aged man still holding the megaphone.

“If that was the grand prize... jackpot.”

[If you win the special prize, treasure will come to you!]

At the corner of the banner the man pointed to, a statue of a golden rooster was vividly printed.

It wasn’t clear how large the real thing was, but based on the detail, it was probably sizable.

“Jackpot.”

“Looks like it’s worth at least 100 don.”

“Does anyone actually win that?”

Well...

To this day, there are no confirmed eyewitnesses of someone winning the special prize.

Exactly.

Considering that guests draw more than half the sticks daily, the odds are statistically absurd.

The writer suspects this is a kind of selective ritual embedded in the disaster itself.

(Branch investigation revealed that 52 years ago, a person said to have drawn the prize ended the village’s drought and was enshrined as a guardian spirit at the local shrine—but no proof exists.)

And just as Agent Choi said—

No one won the special prize this time either.

Disappointment clouded the face of the man holding the megaphone.

[Thank you all once again today! May you be blessed by Jisan!]

And so, the day’s festival drew to a close.

“Aww.”

“Rigged, isn’t it?”

“Come on, at least the drinks and meat were free.”

Outsiders began to get up from their seats. But with so many having drunk free booze, it was tough for many groups to drive home.

“Jeez, this got messy...”

“It’s fine. This person said we can stay the night!”

A good number of outsiders ended up accepting the villagers’ offers and staying overnight, like a makeshift homestay.

It was a fairly warm scene.

But if you looked closely, the villagers were quietly gathering the leftover raffle boxes and whispering among themselves.

They too took turns drawing sticks from the boxes.

After the outsider raffle ends, the villagers gather and begin drawing the remaining wooden sticks from the raffle boxes, one by one.

Until someone finds the “Special Prize” stick.

At this point, they begin chanting an odd phrase:

“O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow.”

“O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow.”

—Probably some kind of ritual incantation or prayer... it doesn’t strictly follow shamanic syntax, but don’t listen too closely. Spells tied to supernatural phenomena almost never have positive effects.

Eventually, one of them finds it.

A stick with gold pressed onto the tip.

“Ah...!”

Tears run down the face of the one who drew it.

The villagers gather around him in celebration, shouting as if in ritual:

“Sansanbaekjisanbokjushiopgedaerijaya!”

“Sansanbaekjisanbokjushiopgedaerijaya!”

Rumor at the branch says whoever draws the special prize among the villagers is assigned a particular role in the next year’s festival.

“......”

I turned my head.

Baek Saheon was watching all of it with sunken eyes.

“Excuse me.”

Baek Saheon glanced toward me.

I asked politely.

“Would there be any place in this village where we might stay the night?”

“...You’re not seriously saying you want to freeload off someone’s food and lodging for free, are you?”

Assistant Manager Eunhaje suddenly cut in, hoisting the rice sack she had won.

“I’ll pay for it. Feels like fate that we met.”

“...Sorry?”

“Here. Will this do?”

She pulled out a hefty stack of cash and held it out to Baek Saheon.

He looked a little taken aback—but his eyes quickly darkened as he snatched the money.

“...Follow me.”

“Okay.”

“Wait! You said you were a reporter? Please leave now. We were the ones who asked—”

“You’re gonna kick out the one who paid? Capitalism’s disgusting, but let’s at least keep it consistent, Grandpa.”

“......”

I heard Agent Cheongdong mutter softly, “Grandpa...?”

God.

I wanted to squeeze my eyes shut. Instead, I silently followed Assistant Manager Eunhaje, who was already walking after Baek Saheon like it was the most natural thing in the world.

“...Would you like me to carry the rice sack?”

“Oh. A young gent repaying the lodging fee, huh? At least someone knows how to show proper courtesy.”

I took the sack from Assistant Manager Eunhaje—her hand must still be injured—and then Agent Cheongdong, looking disgruntled, helped too.

“You’re too late.”

“......”

Agent Cheongdong stared at her with an expression of disbelief.

Then—

“Hey! You goddamn bumpkins! Cucumbers?! You think I came all the way out to this backwater dump for some fucking cucumbers?!”

Thwack!

A box was hurled and smashed nearby. Cucumbers exploded, rolling across the dirt.

A drunk outsider threw a tantrum, kicking and screaming on the ground.

“Gimme gold! I want gold!”

“Oh dear, sir.”

“There are still two more chances, remember!”

The villagers smiled calmly as they comforted him.

...Their faces were unbelievably serene for people who’d just been screamed at.

They promised him dinner, invited him to draw again tomorrow, praised the quality of the local hot spring—and under those kind offers, the rowdy drunk followed them off to someone’s house.

To one of the few tile-roofed houses in the village.

One that sat at the village center, oddly large and imposing.

“......”

I stiffly turned my gaze.

The villagers left behind began quietly packing the broken cucumbers back into the box.

They brushed dirt off each piece, muttering to themselves.

“Sansanbaekjisanbokjushiopgedaerijaya...”

The same incantation-like prayer as before.

“......”

I finally tore my gaze away and kept walking.

“...This way.”

The problem was—

Baek Saheon was heading to that tile-roofed house.

Wait.

Are we seriously going into that clearly suspicious place?

But Agent Cheongdong was moving forward without hesitation, so I didn’t bolt.

...Agent Choi wouldn’t have written a false tip in the help guide.

We neared the house.

“...Come in.”

The entrance was wide, and what now served as the front foyer—a large wooden floor—was equally wide.

But Baek Saheon, as if reluctant to even step on it, moved carefully to the side and opened a door off to one side.

And behind that door stood someone already waiting.

The middle-aged man from before—the one with the megaphone.

“The guests are ready to sleep.”

“Ah, welcome! Haha! Saheon, take good care of them. No funny business.”

“......”

“May you be blessed by Jisan!”

Baek Saheon gave a slow nod and led us inside.

The house was unnaturally large—its layout repetitive, with more and more sliding paper doors appearing as we walked.

Eventually, we reached a corridor lined with aged wooden doors.

Creak.

Baek Saheon opened one.

Inside was a small, lived-in single room.

One neatly folded bedding set, and one dusty spare comforter hastily dragged out.

“You’ll sleep here.”

“Isn’t this your room? Where will you sleep?”

“......”

Baek Saheon gave a look like the question wasn’t worth answering, spun on his heel, and opened another room for Assistant Manager Eunhaje.

Then disappeared down the hall.

“Not much return for the cash, huh?”

Eunhaje shrugged and said casually to me:

“Well then. Sleep well. Let’s see each other tomorrow if we can.”

“...Yes. Thank you.”

I watched her disappear into the adjacent room.

...She definitely recognized Baek Saheon.

And still didn’t acknowledge him.

Because she knew he wouldn’t recognize her.

She knows this ghost tale well.

That meant she was here with a clear objective.

And Assistant Manager Eunhaje...

Is part of Director Ho’s project team.

...What the hell is going on here?

I had several theories, given what I suspected about the extermination-level supernatural disaster Director Ho wanted.

I would have had theories.

But now...

“......”

I was tired.

Of everything.

Enough of that.

Time to think about the wish ticket instead....

“Agent Podo.”

“......”

“Do you remember the mission?”

“Yes.”

This ‘Jisanso Offering Ritual.’

When a rescue request is received, the branch uses the supernatural auto-responder to inform the victim of the time and place to meet.

So we headed to the spot given by the branch.

“This is it.”

Agent Cheongdong showed me the note.

1 a.m.

The village well

The tile-roofed house fell deathly quiet after midnight.

Agent Cheongdong and I slipped out of our rooms in silence and walked.

—After midnight, you’re relatively safe as long as you stay away from the Seonangdang shrine.

But don’t get cocky—no shouting!

“The well’s to the east.”

We crossed the village in the dark, only a few dim streetlamps lighting the way. We passed some houses that looked abandoned, like no one had lived there in ages.

The well was at the center of that area.

A stone-covered well.

And standing beside it, like a guard on watch—

Was Baek Saheon.

“...!!”

His single eye turned toward us.

He spoke calmly.

“You’re agents, aren’t you?”

And in the very next moment—

Agent Cheongdong lunged forward and pinned him.

—If you’re caught by a villager late at night, hide as quickly and quietly as possible.

If that’s not possible?

Subdue them and hide them in an abandoned house!

Just as we were about to drag Baek Saheon toward a derelict house—

“Hey! Isn’t it against the law for civil servants to treat the informant like this?!”

“...!”

Agent Cheongdong froze mid-movement.

“You’re the one who submitted the request?”

“Yes! I’m the one who called this in!”

Baek Saheon tried to shrug off Cheongdong’s grip in frustration and failed—then, glancing nervously between the two of us, quickly lowered his voice.

“Get me out of this village. ...Tonight.”

“It’s a trap.”

Agent Cheongdong immediately turned to me and said aloud:

“Villagers are free to leave once the festival ends. There’s no reason they’d need our help—”

“I can’t leave!”

Baek Saheon panted.

His eyes darted between me and Agent Cheongdong in desperate calculation—then he swallowed hard and spoke.

“...Check my pocket.”

“......”

I reached into the pocket of Baek Saheon’s pants.

Inside was—

A stick, gilded at the tip.

The Special Prize.

“I—I drew it on the first day...”

—Unfortunately, there are no confirmed cases of a villager being rescued from this supernatural phenomenon during the festival period.

If you’re the kind of agent I marked as a promising junior, you’ll want to try saving them anyway, but I’ll say it now:

—Junior. Let the seniors try that. You don’t.

If you try to leave Jisan Village with a villager during the festival...

“Please get me out of here! I’m begging you!”

—The agent disappears too.

Chapter 197

"Please!"

Baek Saheon was practically shrieking.

And yet, even in that state, he still tried to keep his voice as low as possible—as if he only wanted to escape the encroaching supernatural disaster without being noticed.

However, Agent Cheongdong did not release his restraints.

He merely glanced at the gold stick I was holding, the one I had taken from Baek Saheon's pants pocket, and asked a question.

Special Prize.

"What does it mean that you drew that?"

"'What does it mean?' It means I’m fucked, that’s what! That’s why I have to get out of here before the festival labor duty begins. Right now, from here—"

"Do you die?"

Baek Saheon froze.

I interjected and asked again.

"But earlier, the person who pulled that stick was being congratulated. And I heard they get an important role in next year’s event...?"

"......."

Baek Saheon’s lips moved silently, but no words came out.

But his eyes—his eyes were gleaming with fear and fury.

A taboo seal?

I met Agent Cheongdong’s gaze.

The agent gave a slight nod, then asked in an uninflected voice.

"In that case, explain how you obtained the direct line number to headquarters."

"Hah! Isn’t it one of the numbers your people hid all over the village?"

"......!"

"I already know. The whole village knows. We just pretend not to. But how do you even call with that number, huh?"

Baek Saheon’s voice turned derisive, almost spitting.

"Hold on. You were aware of the Agency’s guidance flyers, and still chose to leave them untouched?"

"Yeah! The outsiders drink the Shinsanju and ‘awaken to the truth of the world,’ then hang themselves or drown or rot at the bottom of a well—nobody cares. Whether they slip away or not, it doesn’t matter. They only manage things to avoid spiritual contamination!"

"'Spiritual contamination'? What kind of contamination?"

Baek Saheon clammed up again.

A cold bead of sweat slid down his jaw.

Another taboo.

Watching him crumble under supernatural fear and pressure left me with an odd sinking feeling.

...Because I’m reminded of myself?

But that was only from my perspective.

"So you’re unable to answer."

Agent Cheongdong had no reason to feel sympathy for Baek Saheon.

He was someone who had once killed people in a mountain lodge simply because they possessed a tape.

Because the Disaster Response Agency had designated them as evil.

And Baek Saheon—had a tape.

"If you know that much, then walk out on your own two feet."

"I told you I can’t!"

At the cold remark, Baek Saheon seemed to register the situation and darted his gaze around.

But then, his voice suddenly calmed.

"Hey, you know that thing, right? I know it too. The stone cairn in front of the pine tree. You put a rock on it, pray, and then the agents link arms on either side and walk you out safely from the village."

"......!!"

He was spot on.

If you walk ninety-nine paces east from the well, there’s a dried-up pine tree. In front of it is a stone cairn. You place one more stone atop it.

Then, while reciting ‘Turn back and look after me’ three times, if your party links arms and walks toward the entrance, you’ll return to the everyday world without incident... That’s the current rule.

That’s why it requires at least three people—and why two agents are deployed.

So don’t forget: the agents must link arms at both ends, placing the rescuees in the center!

"I—I've seen it all! Since decades ago, I’ve seen it! That’s how you get rescued, right? Do it for me too! I reported everything! That’s what you’re supposed to do!"

Baek Saheon’s tone was overflowing with desperation and rage.

But...

"That doesn’t work on village residents."

Baek Saheon froze.

"...What?"

"I mean, it won’t work for someone who lives in this village."

Exactly.

Repeating again: this method doesn’t work on villagers participating in the festival, so don’t try it carelessly. If you do it without preparation, you’ll lose your way between the village and reality.

"Villagers can’t escape safely using that method."

"...I don’t live here anymore. I live in Seoul."

Agent Cheongdong said nothing.

Baek Saheon lowered his voice in panic and pleaded.

"I—I only come here once a month, no, once every few months! Just for a few days! I don’t live here! I’m not one of those crazy bastards!"

"The criterion is whether you're wearing a feather from the festival."

"......!"

Baek Saheon's gaze dropped.

To his own chest, where a rooster feather still hung.

"~!!"

He twitched like he was going to rip it off—but quickly froze as if realizing it was impossible.

Then he muttered.

"I can’t?"

"......."

"No way. That can’t be. I’m an adult and I don’t live here. That means I’m an outsider. Then... Then there must be another way. You’re supposed to save citizens. You have to save the one who reported this."

Agent Cheongdong declared coolly.

"As things stand, it’s impossible."

......

......

"Then die."

......

What?

"Then all of you die."

Baek Saheon's mouth opened.

"Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya! Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya! Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya! Sansanbaekjisanbokju—mmph!"

I rushed to clamp a hand over Baek Saheon's mouth.

A sharp pain split through my palm.

"...!"

He bit me.

"Agent Podo!"

"These bastards are stealing the Delegate! Stealing the Delegate! It’s contaminated!"

Agent Cheongdong pulled my hand away, and the moment the pressure left his mouth, Baek Saheon screamed again.

And—

"Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya...."

Figures began to emerge from behind the abandoned house.

No—more than one.

I understood now.

"Please don’t cause such a ruckus in the village."

"That’s right. Ugh, what’s all this in the middle of the night? Our bathwater’s nice and hot—how about a soak and a good rest?"

"Can’t sleep? Maybe your stomach’s not settled."

I finally understood why Agent Choi had warned that if it looked like a villager was going to notice you, you should either hide immediately or overpower and stash them in the abandoned house.

The treatment had changed.

"Time to eat~"

After we were discovered at the well—

The ever-smiling villagers guided us back to a tile-roofed house.

But not to the small, cramped room Baek Saheon had been using—no, they moved our bedding to a spacious, luxurious room with sliding paper doors.

It wasn’t a suggestion. They simply did it.

They offered a bath. Brought out festival food. Alcohol, too.

"Drink up, nice and cold. It’s medicinal wine. Medicinal."

"Brewed with wild greens, herbs, and ginseng from the mountain. Restorative and sharpens the senses~"

Just like the ones who clung to the troublemaker who had thrown a cucumber to the ground.

Baek Saheon was nowhere to be seen.

The villagers had taken him somewhere.

"......."

A chilling premonition passed between our exchanged glances.

Did others who had seen the hidden Disaster Response Agency flyers and called for help... feel this same unease?

The villagers left the food and exited the room. Only the two of us remained. But faint presences still lingered beyond the door.

Then they were gone.

"......."

Agent Cheongdong approached the door and pressed his ear to it.

He waited until he was absolutely sure there was no movement, then carefully reached for the door to peek outside—

Wait.

I grabbed his shoulder, stopping the motion.

Then rummaged through the table, picking up the shallow candle used to keep the food warm.

I held it up near the sliding paper door.

—

Behind it, shadows of tightly packed bodies clung to the other side.

—

"......!"

They had been eavesdropping.

It hadn’t been that they’d left. The presence hadn’t faded.

Fuck.

The chill climbed up my throat and shuddered through me.

I casually set the candle down and tipped over a water cup instead, dipping my fingers in to write silently.

—

Would it be impossible to just knock them all out and leave quietly?

Difficult.

Agent Cheongdong replied quickly.

—

In ghost stories this blatantly religious, sleep is rarely the optimal solution.

"......."

Of course.

This tale must have been observed for a very long time. They must’ve tried all kinds of approaches.

What Agent Choi wrote down for me couldn’t possibly include every variation they’d attempted. Only the safest, most effective ones became formal protocol.

...And not one of them involved rescuing villagers from the disaster.

Even so—

—

How exactly do the outsiders who visit this village during the festival go missing?

—

I had to ask.

But—

—

Unknown.

—

...!!

—

After the festival ends, the villagers always let the outsiders leave.

And there were quite a few reports of people who only stayed one night or insisted on leaving mid-festival who managed to go.

—

But the problem is... some of them never make it home.

—

"......."

—

And even among those who do return safely, quite a few are found to have taken their own lives weeks later.

Most often using natural elements.

—

Chilling.

—I don’t know the exact cause of this situation, but a consistent pattern has been identified.

Entering this ghost story and following the guidance of friendly villagers—accepting their offers of a bath, food, and alcohol.

—It wasn’t clear which of the three was the true trigger, but now we’ve found a hint.

I recalled what Baek Saheon had shouted.

—The outsiders drink Shinsanju, awaken to the truth of the world, then hang themselves, drown, rot at the bottom of a well....

So that’s it.

The alcohol is the certainty.

I looked at the bottle on the table.

Clear liquor in an ordinary white porcelain flask.

But...

If drinking this reveals the truth of the world and leads to death... what does that even mean?

What kind of "truth" could do that?

In many ghost story worlds, "truth" is just a euphemism for hypnosis, brainwashing, mental collapse.

That’s scary enough.

But this—this is even more terrifying.

...A truth that is actually true, and just knowing it makes a person choose death.

A human who learns something they were never meant to know.

...This is bad news.

A chill swept across my chest.

...It would probably be safer to just leave.

My nerves were screaming that digging any deeper would be foolish.

And we still had one certain method left.

The emergency escape tool issued to Agency agents.

The five-colored sneaker laces.

"......."

I had already noticed Agent Cheongdong discreetly peeking under his jacket, clearly torn.

—Are we going to use it now?

Even if the person who reported the incident wasn’t technically one of the victims anymore, we had seen too many people fail to return home after the festival ended.

Though Agent Cheongdong was a stickler for rules, as an agent, he couldn’t ignore that reality.

—If there’s no way to quietly slip out of this room before the festival ends today, I will proceed.

He added, with a tired and guilty expression:

—I apologize. I spoke too harshly and gave the suspect a reason to act out irrationally.

No.

I should’ve known Baek Saheon would act like that....

I’d read so much about him on the wiki, and even lived with him at the staff quarters.

...Must be exhaustion dulling my judgment.

I shook my head and slumped down into a corner of the room.

It was already dawn.

The festival will start soon.

And then—

HiyAAAAAAAH!

As the music and noise of the festival rose outside, building toward a frenzy, we remained trapped inside the room.

They’re not leaving.

Even though the festival had started, someone still came by occasionally to reheat the food and drinks for us.

And there were always at least two people standing outside the door.

They weren’t trying to persuade or mislead us.

Just kind. But clearly, not interested in actually talking to us.

This is overkill.

I was stunned.

What the hell did Baek Saheon tell the villagers about us?

Something wasn’t right.

At this rate, we really might need to tie those laces and just get out of here.

Even Agent Cheongdong seemed to be leaning more toward that option when—

We heard a sound from outside.

"There were people causing a commotion over there... Can I report it here?"

"Yes, yes. They were fighting. Spilled food and—ohhh?"

There was a bit of chatter, then the sound of a crowd rushing off.

And shortly after—

Sliiiiide.

The sliding door opened, and a familiar face appeared.

"So there were people in here."

“...!!”

It was Deputy Eunhaje.

Agent Cheongdong’s eyes widened.

"You...."

"Shh."

The deputy held up a finger to his lips.

Then, scowling, he muttered, "What is this, some human trafficking cult village? They’re all trying to lock up their rogue family members and suspicious outsiders. Anyway, you two want to get out of this room, yeah?"

"Yes!"

"Then let’s go."

Deputy Eunhaje began leading us, skillfully sneaking us out of the tile-roofed house.

Somehow, almost no one was left inside.

"Why is the place so empty all of a sudden?"

"Between festival prep and a big brawl that broke out outside, the villagers all ran off to stop the fight."

"...Did someone, by any chance, incite that fight?"

"Who knows?"

Then Deputy Eunhaje winked.

It was you, wasn’t it?

A strange mix of panic and gratitude welled up inside me—I wanted to scream at him to stop doing dangerous things, but also hug him in thanks.

"Anyway, get going. This village is clearly not normal."

"......Thank you."

"For what?"

And so, guided out through a back exit Deputy Eunhaje had apparently scoped out earlier, we were greeted by the same landscape as yesterday.

[Receive the Blessings of Jisan!]

A village in the middle of its New Year’s festival.

HiyAAAAAAAH!

"Still a complete mess," Deputy Eunhaje muttered, scratching the back of his head as he looked back at us.

"Don’t get caught and dragged back to a room again. In this chaos, you should be able to quietly make it to the entrance without being—"

"Wait."

Agent Cheongdong frowned and cut him off firmly.

"Come with us."

Of course, Deputy Eunhaje looked bewildered.

"What? I told you—I have other business. I have to report on this place to make a living, you know?"

"It’s dangerous."

"You do remember that I’m the one who saved you two from danger?"

Agent Cheongdong sighed. He seemed both frustrated with the delay and genuinely determined to persuade the deputy.

It looked like he was truly concerned that someone who helped them this much might end up going missing. He clearly wanted to get him out safely, if possible.

But as for me...

I’m worried too, but I can’t tell which is more dangerous.

Even if the village seems worse right now, dragging Deputy Eunhaje away could get him punished by Director Ho for slacking off on his assignment.

Which was exactly what happened to me just recently.

Sigh.

My head felt like it was spinning.

And one more thing kept bothering me.

Is it really okay to leave Baek Saheon behind like this?

Of course, Baek Saheon wasn’t going to die—he was going to get promoted all the way to Section Chief. If this was his hometown, and I was just a temporary variable, then nothing happening now would disrupt the established timeline. According to the wiki, he wouldn’t die here.

But... that terror in his eyes was real.

Was it really okay to walk away?

I turned my head instinctively to scan the area—and spotted him immediately.

Baek Saheon.

"......!"

He was still here, carrying food and guiding people—helping with the festival preparations.

His eyes were sunken, but his expression was surprisingly calm.

And just then—

Ding-ding-ding-ding-ding-ding!

[Everyone! It’s time for the lucky draw! Receive your prizes and the Blessings of Jisan!]

The raffle was beginning again.

The villagers moved in unison, carrying the drawing boxes.

"We have to go now."

"I’m telling you—"

Agent Cheongdong’s urgency clashed with Deputy Eunhaje’s protests when—

"Blessings of Jisan upon you!"

The villagers were already spreading out toward the outsiders.

...At this rate, even if we’d sprinted straight for the gate, we would’ve gotten caught.

It was an unstoppable flow.

We were in their sights.

One of them approached us.

Naturally, our turn came almost immediately.

"Blessings of Jisan upon you!"

The raffle box was offered to me.

"......."

Wouldn’t refusing to draw be more suspicious?

There were too many villagers.

Can’t make a scene now.

If we wanted to leave without alerting those from the tile-roofed house, rejecting this and drawing attention to ourselves would be a mistake.

Even if I win, I just won’t take the prize with me when we leave.

After fierce deliberation, I reached out and grabbed the stick nearest me—

And then—

The golden tip glinted.

“...!!”

A stick with golden ends.

The same kind Baek Saheon had stuffed in his pocket yesterday.

Special Prize.

"......."

Suddenly, the world fell into insane silence.

I didn’t need to look to feel the gazes locking onto me.

And then—

"Aaaaaaah!"

"Aaaah!"

[They’ve come! They’ve come! The Blessing of Jisan has arrived!]

O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow.

O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow.

O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow.

O Deputy, may you bestow blessings as white and scattered as mountain snow...

The chant echoed in waves.

I forced myself to tear my eyes from the stick and lift my gaze.

And I saw it.

From across the way—

Baek Saheon, holding the raffle box, grinning with pure ecstasy.

Chapter 198

The villagers rushed in.

[He has come, the Blessing of Jisan!]

The voice of the one holding the megaphone grew louder and louder. Before long, the sounds of songs and small gongs violently revived.

A whirlpool of people.

“This way!”

I followed Agent Cheongdong and slipped into a gap, trying to hide from the villagers—but it wasn’t enough.

“Wow, unbelievable.”

“Is he the one who’s getting the golden rooster?”

Whispers from those who hadn’t yet grasped the situation. Villagers throwing everything aside and swarming toward me. People falling prostrate on the ground and praying toward the mountain. Baek Saheon vanishing amidst it all.

And then—

“Run.”

I grabbed Agent Cheongdong, who had pushed Deputy Eunhaje and me back and now looked ready to stand against the villagers.

“Calm down. At this point, my chances of getting out ‘safely’ are very low.”

“......!”

“And it was definitely in the rulebook.”

We locked eyes.

“About what to do in a situation like this.”

Occasionally, during extraction operations, one of the agents might get captured by villagers.

But unless it's the final day of the festival, do not charge in or engage the entire village.

Villages mid-festival can always be re-entered. That means you can return, fully prepared.

Don't let your emotions take over and throw away a rare opportunity with nothing but chaos. Act wisely, and boldly.

The captured, the escaped, and the would-be rescuers must all survive and escape the disaster.

“Go.”

“......”

“Go, and quickly figure out a way to rescue me. Come back. That’s how I survive.”

I could see the fierce conflict in Agent Cheongdong’s eyes. The struggle between emotion and rational judgment.

But soon, reason won.

“Tomorrow.”

“......”

“I’ll return without fail.”

I quickly tucked the Five-Colored Sneaker Laces he handed me into the Inventory Tattoo.

In the worst-case scenario, it was the card I’d use to escape—risking disappearance or even suicide.

And Agent Cheongdong tore through the crowd with terrifying speed, sprinting toward the exit.

“He has come! The Blessing of Jisan!”

At the same time, the villagers swarmed me.

I blinked.

I must’ve lost consciousness for a moment.

And when I came to... I was on the wide, antique wooden floor of a traditional room.

Sliding paper doors on two sides of the square space.

“......”

I’d seen this layout recently.

That tiled-roof house.

Deputy Eunhaje was nowhere to be seen. ...He must not have been captured with me.

That was a relief.

“......”

I looked down at my legs.

Both ankles were tied with a sacred golden rope.

The rope trailed through a hole in one of the sliding doors and extended outside.

When I tugged slightly, it resisted. It was anchored firmly.

This is serious.

Just then—

Drrrrk.

The sliding door opened, and people entered.

No—just two.

One carrying a small tray of festival food, the other holding a bottle of liquor. They bowed deeply before setting the clean, elaborate meal and drink before me.

And then faint music began to play.

Outside, it sounded like a frenzied festival was raging all night long.

“......”

The two lifted a small raffle box and each drew a stick. The one who pulled the rod adorned with a rooster feather remained; the other bowed and quietly exited.

The one who remained was the man who had brought the liquor.

“Saheon-ssi.”

“......”

Baek Saheon—the villager who brought the liquor—looked at me.

The likely suspect behind why I suddenly drew the Special Prize.

“Was it you who deliberately made me draw the ‘Special Prize’ from my pocket last night?”

...Check my pocket.

He’d definitely said that to me naturally—leading me there.

The only suspicious moment explaining how someone like me, who had pulled blanks the day before, could now draw the Special Prize.

“You made it so I’d already become someone who had drawn the Special Prize, so that I’d be seen as a ‘suitable’ choice?”

“......”

“And then, you guided me into drawing it again the next day.”

A flash of deep satisfaction crossed Baek Saheon’s eyes.

Then, in a hoarse voice, he said:

“What if that’s true?”

“You planned this for a long time.”

“......”

“Up to predicting how this might work out, and what to do if the agents failed to rescue you.”

A smile of ecstatic release spread across Baek Saheon’s lips.

“That’s right.”

Liberation.

Madness.

“I thought about it every day. What would I do if I won someday? I never wanted to end up like that. Every damn night in that fucked-up house, every time someone died, even when my sister died!”

He spat the words out like vomit.

“I told myself I would never end up like that. Fuck, if I’m going to die—god or not—”

“God?”

Baek Saheon swallowed hard. A mix of dread and pleasure crossed his face—the thrill of speaking a forbidden word.

“This holiday festival is a ritual to serve a god, isn’t it.”

“Serve? No!!”

Baek Saheon screamed the word.

“This is a ritual to create a god!”

“......!”

“They say: ‘Pick an auspicious day and turn it into a holiday. Enshrine the chosen winner in the Seonang Shrine, and from there, create a god—the Blessing of Jisan who will liberate us from worldly truth.’ They actually believe that crazy bullshit and go through with it!”

“Is that even possible?”

“I don’t know! But...”

Panting.

“There’s something real going on here.”

“......”

“If no outsider draws the Special Prize during the holiday, then we collect the leftover sticks and draw amongst ourselves. And if someone wins from that...”

Baek Saheon’s face hardened.

“When the final day of the festival ends, they blindfold the winner like an animal for slaughter and drag them to the Seonang Shrine.”

“......”

“And then inside the shrine, they do something.”

“What do they do?”

“I don’t know. I really don’t. But... but there’s something.”

His face turned pale.

“When the ones who got dragged in come out in the morning, their faces are all different. They can’t speak. Then they start helping prepare for the next year’s holiday...”

“......”

“And when the holiday they helped prepare ends, they hang themselves in the shrine.”

...!

“But then, every holiday... they come back. Even though they’re dead, they come back. They walk out of the Seonang Shrine.”

He wailed.

“They play the festival music!!”

...!

“They’re doing it now. Outside!”

Hiyahhhhhh!

“I won’t end up like that. I won’t die. No way.”

Baek Saheon’s eyes gleamed—fear, rage, the instinct to survive, and the relief of having succeeded—before gradually cooling into rational calculation.

“Now that you’ve drawn the winning stick, the holiday and the lottery are both over. I don’t have to come back here anymore. No more being trapped in Jisan Village. It’s all over.”

......

“I understand.”

I nodded.

Baek Saheon muttered.

“So now you can talk because you’re an insider too? Haha. ...I used to think guys who blabbed about this stuff first were all dumbasses, but this... this is what it feels like?”

A situation only one person has experienced. The kind of secret information you’re forced to swallow without speaking.

It’s funny, but I might understand what Baek Saheon is feeling right now more than anyone else.

......

To be honest, I probably should’ve been angry or panicked right now.

This insane bastard Baek Saheon just shoved a government agent—someone who came here to save him, who only said there was no way to rescue him—into the role of sacrificial lamb so he could save himself.

I should’ve been furious, disgusted, sick with anxiety over what had happened to me, brain spinning to figure out how to escape.

That would’ve made sense.

But strangely, that wasn’t how I felt.

Instead, weirdly enough... I felt at ease.

A slight haze cast over tomorrow and the near future.

Since I’m trapped here, I don’t have to think about anything else right now.

No more worrying about the wish ticket. Or Director Ho’s taboo seal. Or the intel I had to gather within the month. Or what to do with the agent whose identity had already been exposed.

This is just straight-up escapism, isn’t it.

It’s kind of absurd, kind of pathetic, to find peace in the threat of death.

But it was true.

Which is why I was able to speak plainly.

No sympathy. No anger.

“That’s fine. I understand.”

“Understand?”

Suspicion returned to Baek Saheon’s eyes.

“Pity, empathy, ‘I understand’... is this the part where you try to say it’s wrong and beg me to let you go?”

“Maybe. But... spending each day afraid you’re going to die, unable to feel safe, with no peace in your mind.”

“......”

“I know what that’s like.”

He looked at me with a subdued expression.

“Still, you got lucky, picking the right one of the two agents.”

“What, you want to die or something?”

......

“Maybe a little?”

Right now, I wasn’t sure myself.

“Haven’t you ever wanted to die? Not even once?”

“......”

“You have, haven’t you.”

“That’s not happening.”

Baek Saheon spoke abruptly.

“I don’t want to die. If you want to die, good for you. Then just stay where you are.”

“......”

How strange.

There wasn’t a hint of mockery in his voice, like he was trying to ridicule me for falling into his trap. He didn’t even seem amused despite clearly having the upper hand.

All I could sense was his selfish desperation to survive.

He didn’t seem to feel guilty either, but it wasn’t that he was shameless—he just didn’t have the capacity to afford guilt.

Now that I think about it, he’s always been like this.

Only ever scrambling for his own benefit, his own survival.

Still, if he wasn’t someone who got a kick out of watching others suffer because of him... then there might be another way to approach him.

Something I could use.

...No matter what, I can’t just keep hiding from reality until I die.

Eventually, I opened my mouth.

“Saheon-ssi. If there’s a way for both of us to live—would you help me?”

“...What?”

“It’ll be harder for you than it is now. And you’ll have to take a risk that you’ll be exposed.”

“You serious? Are you insane?”

That was when it happened:

—Heavens, that’s enough! There’s a limit to what counts as dramatic tension! How could someone be so rude?!

—Ah, my friend. These days it seems everyone repays kindness with betrayal! Even Brown’s heart aches.

...The plush doll had started speaking again.

—I’ll help you. First, let’s get out of here.

—After that, let’s give them a taste of what karma really is. Won’t that be deliciously thrilling?

.......

Ah. It’s okay.

The plush doll fell silent.

Let me try doing it alone. Won’t that be more fun?

—Well... I suppose. It would flow better with the right assist, though!

It’s fine.

I ended the mental exchange with the plush doll and looked back at Baek Saheon.

I wasn’t expecting anything from him, so the words came out easily.

“But Saheon-ssi. I’m going to die because of you.”

“......”

“I came here to rescue you. I failed because there was no way. But it’s because you pushed me into this position that I’ll die.”

“How the hell is this my fault?”

Baek Saheon’s face twisted.

“Did I tell them to kill someone if I drew the fucking Special Prize? Did I drag people into the shrine and kill them? The ones doing the killing are someone else! What, should I have just died instead? That—”

Cursing and anger bring misfortune.

“......!!”

Baek Saheon and I both turned toward the sliding paper doors.

A murmuring could be heard.

Cursingandangerbringmisfortune. Cursingandangerbringmisfortune. Cursingandangerbringmisfortune.

Forgetfulone, begone. Forgetfulone, begone. Forgetfulone, begone.

Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya. Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya. Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya.

The voices echoed.

Baek Saheon, pale as chalk, stared toward the door.

There was nothing there—but the sound of the festival percussion grew louder and louder...

“Baek Saheon-ssi.”

“No—”

He stumbled to the other side of the room, crouching and covering his head.

It was hard to believe this was the same guy who’d casually pulled off reckless stunts so many times before.

A trauma response?

I wondered if this might work.

I hesitated for a moment, then spoke.

Toward the door.

“I apologize. I cursed because I was startled.”

The noise stopped.

“That was an inappropriate attitude for the festival. I’ll try to speak more politely from now on.”

The sliding door remained motionless, quiet as death.

And then—

Hiyahhhhhh!

The festival percussion started again—and slowly began to fade.

Until it completely dissolved into the background festival noise.

“......”

“......”

“Baek Saheon-ssi.”

He stood up from the corner of the room, drenched in cold sweat.

And looked at me with disoriented eyes.

“I just saved your life again.”

“......”

“Don’t you feel even a little bit grateful?”

“...Are you gonna ask me to die in your place just because I said thanks?”

“You speak like saying thank you comes with a massive price tag.”

Baek Saheon looked at me like I’d just said the most ridiculous thing.

So be it.

“Suit yourself. You seem to value your life quite highly.”

“But I value my life too. Even if I sometimes think about dying.”

“......”

“......”

A long silence.

Then Baek Saheon finally spoke.

“What exactly is this method where both of us live?”

“......”

I slipped my free hand into my pocket and rolled the silver badge between my fingers.

Silver Heart.

“It’s simple.”

Because just now—listening to Baek Saheon—I realized something.

What kind of ghost story this is.

“I’m going to plant doubt in their beliefs.”

Creating a god. Liberation from worldly truth. Coincidence and fate. A sacrificial ritual shared by only a few. The return of the dead. The offering...

Everything pointed to one thing.

This was an urban legend under the jurisdiction of the Brilliant Nameless Sect.

Chapter 199

The Jisan Village festival continued through the night.

Outsiders were allowed to rest in any of the fully opened private homes, and they could enjoy alcohol and meat for free until dawn.

In the end, quite a few of them chose not to leave, remaining in the village.

“What’s going on?”

“Oh, I heard there’s some big event on the last day. Like... some kind of shaman ritual?”

“What’s that?”

“Don’t know. But I’m gonna film it and make Shorts content.”

Some outsiders idly stayed up all night muttering to each other, while others drunkenly passed out in random rooms.

And then—on the morning of the final day.

“Oh.”

“Looks like it’s starting now.”

[The Blessing of Jisan is coming~]

At last, the ritual began.

Doom doom doom

To the beat of janggu drums played by the festival musicians, the villagers lined up on both sides, creating a long corridor-like space.

From the tiled-roof house—

To the Seonang Shrine.

And finally, from the tiled-roof house at the village center—the starting point of that path—something emerged.

[Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya.]

It was a massive human palanquin.

Four people, rooster feathers stuck into their chests and heads covered with plain cloth, were carrying someone.

“Oh.”

“Yo, this is getting real.”

The outsiders, merely curious, exchanged indifferent remarks. But hidden among them were those whose eyes gleamed in silence.

What a mess.

Deputy Eunhaje.

The man once addressed by Kim Soleum as such observed the movements of the palanquin-bearers and villagers from afar.

At this rate, it seemed the project’s mission could be carried out without obstruction.

His sharp gaze turned outward.

Toward the area filled with abandoned houses—toward the well.

The well.

That was the true objective of his mission.

The agents of the Disaster Response Agency had yet to determine exactly why the villagers were less aggressive near the well, or why it was comparatively safe.

They’d investigated it outside festival periods, but found only a blocked, disused well.

But this “reporter” knew the exact reason. He knew what he had to investigate.

That well had once been a passage connected to somewhere else.

And during the bizarre duration of this festival, the village itself transformed into a high-grade anomaly—sometimes reactivating that passage’s function.

Which is why it had to be investigated now.

But—

“......”

The reporter glanced at Kim Soleum, riding atop the human palanquin, then quietly began to move.

Not toward the well between the abandoned houses—

But in the opposite direction.

[Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya]

Meanwhile, the procession continued.

The festival musicians parted the way, the palanquin passed, and the villagers bowed in worship. Others stumbled along behind it in fervent devotion.

But they kept a certain distance. As though getting too close was not permitted.

The problem was—some among them didn’t care whether it was permitted or not.

“Whoa! Check this out—it’s the first time they’ve held this in decades!”

Some outsiders followed close behind, shoving smartphones in for a better view.

The drunken and dull-witted ignored the invisible boundaries, even tapping the hooded palanquin-bearers on the shoulder.

They’d only come to this countryside festival for the free food and booze anyway.

But the villagers didn’t stop them.

They only stared with calm, smiling faces.

“Wow, not bad for a backwater town.”

“Professionalism. Real pros.”

The villagers did not flare up.

They simply watched—nervously, earnestly—ensuring the palanquin’s safe arrival.

“......”

At last, the palanquin arrived safely at the Seonang Shrine and came to a stop.

The four bearers cautiously lowered the “chosen one” they had carried on their shoulders.

Two of them stepped toward the shrine and cut the golden rope.

Snip.

The rope dropped to the ground. The remaining two removed the rope tied to the chosen one’s feet and rehung it on the shrine.

Then the shrine’s door was opened.

Inside was...

“The door’s opening! Whoa! Look at that! Gold!”

A golden rooster.

That golden rooster statue—the one that represented the “Special Prize”—had been enshrined inside the Seonang Shrine all along.

But the size of it was enormous.

“Is that not just gold-plated?”

“Holy shit.”

In the center of the shrine—where an altar should have been—was a large round wooden platform, and atop it stood a structure nearly the size of a human.

The outsiders’ expressions morphed from awe into something far more primal—greed.

They barely registered the pole behind the golden statue—jammed in upside-down in the floor of the shrine.

Just like the sticks jammed upside-down inside the raffle box.

[Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya]

The one who had been riding the palanquin finally moved.

Dressed in a white robe, they stood before the shrine. One by one, villagers approached and placed paper sheets atop the robe.

The papers were thick with ink, crowded with writing.

Korean characters. Chinese characters.

The names of villagers.

And after bowing three times, they poured liquor over the robe.

Sluuurrrp.

The Shinsanju flowed, washing away the black ink and causing the names to vanish.

It looked as if the liquid were soaking into the person clad in white.

“Liberate us from worldly truth, O Blessing of Jisan, O Proxy.”

“Liberate us from worldly truth, O Blessing of Jisan, O Proxy.”

“Liberate us from worldly truth, O Blessing of Jisan, O Proxy.”

The villagers bowed. They prayed. They wept. They screamed, vomiting up old, deep-seated terrors, raising both arms or begging toward the heavens.

The despair and desperation couldn’t be drowned out, even by the music of the festival troupe.

“Uh...”

“Hey. This feels kind of off, doesn’t it?”

Most of the outsiders began to awkwardly back away.

They were starting to sense something strange.

And then—

“Let’s get a closer look at this gold statue. Oh wait, what’s that... it smells kinda nice... like alcohol...”

Thunk.

It was the moment a smartphone-wielding outsider set foot inside the shrine.

“Huh?”

[Sansanbaekjisanbokjusiopgedaeirijaya]

The golden rooster began to melt.

Bubbling.

As though sizzling, the statue melted and seeped into the grooves in the floorboards...

And soon touched the foot of the one who had dared to enter.

That golden liquid.

“......”

The outsider looked down at their foot in a daze. Their mouth opened.

“I see it now!”

Thud.

Their smartphone fell to the floor.

“The world is ■■! We’re nothing but ■■ scribbled within it!”

“Please don’t look at me don’t look at me don’t ■■ me—”

“I have value I’m fascinating no?? I’m not?? What is this what?? My name, sir?”

The madness spread like contagion.

“What... what the hell?”

“What’s wrong with them?”

Those at the edges gasped at the sight, recoiling—or craning their necks for a better view.

But it was already too late.

“AAAH!”

Most of them slipped into delirium.

Screams. Incoherent rambling. People slumped with blank, empty eyes.

Madness and silence coexisted.

But the villagers didn’t look back at them.

They’d seen it all too many times—those who touched the Shinsanju, glimpsed the truth of the world, and were consumed by its terror.

Outsiders were outsiders, nothing more.

And they—the family of Jisan Village—would not share their fate.

Because this, this long Jisan Festival they had so painstakingly prepared, was finally approaching its end—

[He is coming!]

Only those unlike these defiled ones—those who had served the ritual with devotion for decades, who had awaited the Blessing of Jisan as the Chosen—only they would receive liberation!

“A new pole is being erected at the Seonang Shrine!”

The clamor of the kkwaenggwari gongs drowned the frenzy.

The chosen one began calmly walking toward the Seonang Shrine—unbothered by the chaos.

Still holding the golden stick, the Special Prize.

All it would take was to drive that small stick into the golden liquid now pooled beneath the shrine, and the chosen one would melt into it, becoming the Blessing of Jisan.

A sacrifice—no, a god—who would deliver liberation from that terrifying truth of the world.

The villagers believed that they, at last, would escape this monstrous and brutal world, avoid misfortune, and be freed.

But then...

.......

“Everyone.”

A flat, bureaucratic voice.

“There’s a procedural error here.”

“...?!”

“The Blessing of Jisan speaks falsehoods to abandon us!”

“Doubtful.”

Toward the sky.

“I know.”

He spoke in their language:

“I have been cleansed by the Shinsanju and awakened to the truth of the world. I already know what kind of Blessing of Jisan should raise the golden pole at the shrine.”

The truth.

“You twisted the teachings. Perhaps you read a few random pages of the Nameless Scripture somewhere, or heard parts of it and misunderstood.”

Confusion.

Whispers. Groans from those who didn’t understand.

“What did you believe liberation from worldly truth was? Fleeing from this cruel, painful, unjust world? Escaping misfortune and living as chosen exceptions under the Blessing’s protection?”

The villagers lifted their heads.

An answer, through action.

“I see. In that case, let’s begin here.”

Kim Soleum spoke.

With the certainty of knowledge.

The most important doctrine of the Brilliant Nameless Sect.

The First Teaching.

“There is no freedom in this world.”

“There is no escape from the Name.”

.......

Silence as heavy as a corpse.

No.

No.

Impossible!

“—was me.”

The disappearances of outsiders. The sacrificial lottery. The living offerings.

From all corners came cries:

“No!”

“It can’t be!”

“We’re defiled—this is defilement!”

But they could not kill the one before them.

If he were to die within this village, he would become a new god.

That was the procedure.

In the end... they had no choice but to let him go.

“Aaaagh!”

The musicians staggered, and people collapsed all around.

The village festival ended in chaos and confusion.

And the chosen one—Kim Soleum, who had steered it all to this conclusion using his specialty in information warfare and psychological manipulation—

As expected.

Watched it all unfold with sunken eyes.

Without joy.

I knew this would happen.

I looked around at the insane spectacle.

Yes, this was indeed a ghost story under the jurisdiction of the Brilliant Nameless Sect. But it wasn’t a proper site of their doctrine.

Probably on purpose.

“This village was ‘experimented on’—given rituals just accurate enough to let the ghost story take form.”

To fully understand this process, I would need Baek Saheon’s help.

I had to figure out how exactly the ritual worked, and what teachings they truly followed.

And—

Baek Saheon had manipulated another villager who’d drawn a golden stick to buy me enough hours in the dawn to hear everything and analyze it.

...Did he pick up another brainwashing artifact in the meantime?

I no longer had the will to retrieve it.

It didn’t matter. Not anymore.

Anyway—thanks to that, I was able to pinpoint their mistakes.

And since all of my knowledge of the Brilliant Nameless Sect was authentic, the villagers had been all the easier to manipulate.

As for me—having long suspected what this so-called “truth of the world” really was—I was immune to its influence.

...I mean, I read all this on the wiki already.

In any case—

“Jisan Village will now be trapped in its own schism, torn between what is right and what is not, each side believing what they want to believe.”

And once that division breaks their unity, weaknesses will emerge.

The SDRA should be able to intervene before next year’s holiday.

So before the chaos settled down—

I’d slip away.

The festival was over.

The village had rejected me as the chosen one.

Which meant... it should be safe to leave now.

“......”

Splat.

That had been the golden rooster.

Judging by the color, it looked like the circular wooden platform beneath it hadn’t melted.

Probably still resting on the floor.

Only the gold had melted, huh...

Thud.

“......”

Thud.

The ground trembled. No—

Thud.

The floor.

“...!”
